        I would also recommend the propriety of making certain modificad°L
     in the present pre-emption law, which may contribute to carry out, in a
     liberal spirit, the beneficent policy of government in the enactment of sd ,
        The first modification I would suggest is the extension of the pre-^,
   ■ tion principle to all settlers on unsurveyed public land, after the exting1115^
     ment of the Indian tide. This change is called for by consideration3
     justice and policy.           / '      ;
   ; The emigration to the west is increasing so rapidly, that the settlem^
   •on the frontiers are extending far in advance of the public surveys. " j
     men who form those settlements are justly entitled to the protection^
     favor of government; and yet, strange as it may .appear, the whole of
     class is, by existing legislation, excluded from the privileges of pre empn° J
     while claimants of no greater merit, under the Rio Hondo settlement *
     Florida armed occupation act, are limited by no such restrictions.
        I consider the spirit of the pre emption principle violated, when
     settlers, who open the way for succeeding emigration, are deprived of
     benefit of their settlements because they have been made on unsurv^V^
     public land ; and despoiled of their homes, perhaps, by the very men         J
    they have pioneered into the country, merely because the latter hapPe jjf
    to become the first settlers on the land after it is surveyed. The
  •this respect tends to reward cupidity and favor spoliation, and ought
    modified at once, in such a manner as to prevent the commission of sl
  'flagrant injustice.      •                    ;
 * The second change I would recommend, is the extension of the
    pre-emption to settlers on surveyed land whose settlements comn^11^
    prior to 1st June, 1840. The act of4lh September, 1841, only makes pr°rg,
    ion for settlements commenced since the 1st June, 1840 ; while prior
    who cannot secure their rights under-the acts of 1838 and 1840, in c° 00
   quence of the restrictive provisions of those acts, are excluded fr011^ $
    privilege of pre emption, and their improvements are liable to be
    others, who may have settled on the premises subsequent to theTst of 1
    1840.' The first settler, who continues to inhabit the land, should
   ferred in all such cases, and the law should be so modified as to al'0'
   the privilege of entering his improvement by pre emption.
 ; ‘ My third recommendation is, to extend to owners and residents 00
   the right of pre emption to so much adjoining or neighboring land            00
   be necessary for fuel, fencing, and other similar purposes. In s0°^prs a1^

      He was about to close his labors for the season, and return to his
   and friends in this city, to arrange his geological information, calculate
   barometrical observations, and do the office work necessary to complete 1
   return of his surveys to you, when a violent storm wrecked his boat on .
   rocks near Eagle river, on Point Keewenaw, and he, with two of his b°
   men, perished.                                                            r the
      He had in his boat, at the time of his death, the,field notes oi
   subdivision of three or four fractional townships, which were lost with W
   The remainder of his field notes and geological and barometrical obscr

   office as soon as his administrators can have them written out, an^fuat

   purpose. This will require considerable labor, and cannot be done by
   other person as well as it would have been done by himself, had he b
   permitted to live to complete it; but enough can be done to show
   the great value of such accurate geological and topographical surveys aS
   one in which he was engaged, and to demonstrate the practicability^
   carrying them on, in connexion with the ordinary linear surveys of
   public lands, without increasing the expenditures of the government jy
✓ surveying, more than half a cent per acre, even'in a rough and tffic
   wooded country like that on lake Superior.                  .                ai^
      In a country of prairies and open woodland, where the barometer
   other necessary instruments can be carried with facility and safety? ^
   increase of expenditure, over and above the cost of the ordinary sur5^
   need not exceed one-fourth of a cent per acre, or say one dollar per n111
    the exterior boundaries of townships, and fifty cents per mile for the in       f
   or sectional,lines ; which would amount to about one-Jifth of a cem r
    acre.                                                        .            ^0
 '     The additional information which such surveys woulcf give
    certainly be worth to the purchasers of the public lands far more than .(5
   small sum in any part of the United States ; and in a mining regi^ ^0
    value and importance would be greatly increased. It would enabt -y
    government to know at once the exact location and probable value 01
    section and quarter section of the mineral lands; and to bring into #
    as settlers may require them, all those lands which are only valua
    farming purposes.                                    -                    erior?
       This latter consideration, in a mining district like that on lake
    is a matter of much importance; for, between the mineral ranged ^lii^
    country, there are large tracts of good farming lands, which have no .
    for mining purposes, but would sell readily to persons who would ?anejgb*
    them, and who would thus be enabled to supply the miners in their
    borhood with food.                           '                 ~       afor1*^

              REPORT OF THE SURVEYOR GENERAL OF FLORIDA.

                    -/             Surveyor General’s Office,
                                   ,  • .     St. Augustine, Oct. 2Qth. 1845.
  WIR : Prefatory to giving details of my. annual report, I take this oc­
  ti/11 of again advising you that, under my instructions, I reached this
  to a      lbe night of the 29th ult., when I expected to meet my predecessor,
        /er over the archives and furniture of the office, as required by
  y/instructions to him, under date of the 14th August last, a copy of which
  uy“fwarded me for my government. The office clerk handed me a let-
  b, POffi that gentleman, informing that from family reasons, and other
  1 ^ltless> he would not meet me, and that his clerk would deliver the office.
      as placed in possession on the morning of the 2d inst., since when I
      6 been diligently engaged in examining the records, correspondence,
  )oi &c,> with a view to comprehend fully its present situation. I refer
  V ’o my letters of the 4th, 8th, 11th, and 18th inst., explanatory of my
 ' jarches.                  ■              .           _
  ^hd atl1 advised that a surveyor of private land claims has been appointed,
       e,3’ered into bond with my predecessor, and has been twice in the field
  lg?®r three several instructions, viz: the first of date 25th November,
          from this office; second, from the department direct, (copy sent this
  KCe>) of date.25th March, 1845, and lastly from this office, under date 1st
         1$45, wherein was pointed out the manner his duties were to be. per-
  PrOdB' 1 regret to advise you that his labors up to this period have been
         Ct’ve of little or no good, as the'work deposited in the office has not
        °®c>ally received by my predecessor, from the alleged reason of non-
                with the instructions above referred to, and which seem to be
 X? 6 m their details. This subject shall meet my early attention, and
 ’tall gentleman again be sent to the field, with such lights afforded practi-
b        111 the office, as will insure permanent results to his labors.
 Betti c°nformity with general instructions, and the requirement of your
 BettZ? ’’^der date Sth ult., I have the honor to report triplicate diagrams
 itoen d A, with explanatory notes showing the entire surveys which have
 (al$0 .Executed in Florida, since the establishment of this office, in 1824;
 And ./se about to be executed under contracts made by my predecessor,
  ’et/l °Se now estimated for, the completion of which in Abe fields is con-
I l)oated by the close of the fiscal year ending 30th June, 1847.
jbent • ument B shows what contracts were entered into by the late incum-
       t£ Jhe years 1844,' 1845, which have been reported to this office dur-
heyle totter year, and designating those fully certified to by the late sur-
i I)q’a^d those wholly or in part suspended.                           .
jday         meat C shows the contracts entered into and on file, on the 28th ,
lotted toly last, duplicates of which, appear to have been regularly trans-
| iloc y°Ur office. I am advised they are not yet m progress.
                   D shows my estimate in detail for the fiscal year ending on
        U h June, 1847.                   '
                                   •        •
   [ 16 ]
         Document E is omitted, not having received any funds for disbursement
         In closing this annual report, I deem it a duty I owe to my country""
      to its constituted officers, and to the interested portion of my fellow-citizeJ1S
      in Florida and elsewhere—to call the attention of Congress, through tfl£!
     regular channel of communications, to the great and increasing difficult
     of locating the confirmed private land claims in the land district east>
     Those of the district west have long since been happily disposed of
     my official action. These difficulties arise from many causes, soid^
     which I respectfully set forth as follows: The. vague and imperfect
     for beginning corners in most of the grants, absence of original sur^5’
     and, where executed, mostly irregular. Disinclination on the part oftb®
 • claimants, after due notice given, to appear and establish their star1111’
     points. Onerous detention and heavy expenses imposed on the def01'
     surveyors, in waiting within their respective districts for these evidei1^
     The assistant surveyor being a salaried officer, his pay and expenses
     corps and outfit devolve upon the government. The locating claim5 ,
    them without sufficient data. The complaints of the owners after
    laid down, that they are wrong, and supplicating new surveys, and Wn
    done, requiring the alteration of every map containing them, in the sev
    offices, to again define them—thus retarding the public surveys thro^
    interminable labors on the officers and clerks of the several offices
    nected therewith ; and when the land is surveyed as public domain, i[ ,e
    comes open to settlers, and a new source of difficulty arises. These nr.£
   some of the evils bearing on this subject, and I would respectfully su&
   the propriety of Congress taking up this matter, and passing an actfL
   viding, when claims are thus indefinite, the claimants failing on due j,.
     ice to establish their beginning corners, and the land is surveyed aS
   he land, and returned to the respective offices, that the claimants
   nutted to enter in the register’s office, agreeably to the proper subdiv'^f               /
   of mp public land, in compact form, the amount in acres (as near a*                 I ,
   be) of their respective claims, and as contiguous as possible to the pla^ Lt
   tended for their locations, and thus obtain their patents. Otherw^ teS
   being able to substantiate their claims either in the field or United
   court, they will lose them.                                                       J
       This seems to me a safe and speedy course to dispose of this di^Ll I,
   subject-one that hangs an incubus on our land system, and fettered                      I
  us by the want of a proper system m East Florida, in granting l^O:
  and not locating a large portion of the land claims by the Spanish               -L
  ties.                                                 *                        «
‘ In the performance of another duty, I feel some delicacy in calMi'
  attention of Congress, through the department, to a simple fact in                      f
  to the salaries of the surveyor general and clerks in this office, os Xndl
  inadequate to the support of themselves and their respective families 1
 the undivided attention to their duties being almost ever necessary              Pl
 up the business of the office, they cannot look to other sources                     I
 derive support, without being impugned in their official stations. Is ’1 tS! !
 then reasonable to hope^ that Congress will grant, from the know11 '                   |
 above stated, a proper and salutary relief? And to this end, I wotd ri1 ’
 g®st> with a view of performing all the duties, incident to this offi^                 j
 i^ke?oEnEr’.as                    by my letter on your files, under date
 14th, 1841, that the following estimate be laid before that body, i°r                  4
                                                                                       [161
     $ thkVat’°n and acfi°n> and thereby secure the services of talented clerks
